Global renormalized solutions to Boltzmann systems modeling mixture gases of monatomic and polyatomic species Thanks: ∗ Corresponding author August 10, 2026
Yi-Long Luo, Jing-Xin Nie ∗

Abstract
. Inspired by DiPerna-Lions’ work [ 17 ] , we study the renormalized solutions to the large-data Cauchy problem of the Boltzmann systems modeling mixture gases of monatomic and polyatomic species, in which the distribution functions f α f_{\alpha} characterized the polyatomic species contain the continuous internal energy variable I ∈ ℝ + I\in\mathbb{R}_{+} . We first construct the smooth approximated problem and establish the corresponding uniform and physically natural bounds. Then, by employing the averaged velocity (-internal energy) lemma, we can show that the weak L 1 L^{1} limit of the approximated solution is exactly a renormalized solution what we required. Moreover, we also justify that the constructed renormalized solution subjects to the entropy inequality. Keywords. Renormalized solution, Existence, Boltzmann equation, weak L 1 L^{1} compactness MSC2020. 76P05, 82C40, 35Q20, 45K05, 35A01



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
Model for mixture gases of monatomic and polyatomic species

In 1975, Borgnakke and Larsen
[
13
]
established a rarefied flow (a Boltzmann collision model) for a gas mixture of polyatomic molecules possessing internal energy, where the related ideas were followed from the works of Morse
[
44
]
, Wang Chang-Uhlenbeck
[
48
]
and Chapman-Cowling
[
14
]
. The polyatomicity is modeled by a continuous internal energy variable
I\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Compared to the classical mechanical models (i.e., monatomic gases models) such as rough-spheres, loaded spheres and various
C
-bodies, or special intermolecular potentials, which do not possess the continuous internal variables, the Boltzmann collision integral for diatomic or polyatomic gases is of more complexity, which will be illustrated by geometric intuitivity as follows. For instance, while considering the elastic collision for two monatomic molecules with the same unit mass (without internal energy variable), the conservation laws of momentum and energy read

\displaystyle\xi+\xi_{*}=\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{*}^{\prime}\,,\ |\xi|^{2}+|\xi_{*}|^{2}=|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+|\xi_{*}^{\prime}|^{2}\,,

where
(\xi,\xi_{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{*}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
are respectively pre-collision and post-collision velocities of two monatomic molecules, see Grad’s work
[
27
]
and the references therein. As shown in Figure
1
, in the cross section of the corresponding collision, the four “points”
(\xi,\xi_{*},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{*}^{\prime})
form a rectangle.

\xi^{\prime}
\xi_{*}^{\prime}
\xi
\xi_{*}

Figure 1.
Transformation of velocities for monatomic molecules with same mass.

However, while considering the collision for two polyatomic molecules with same unit mass, the conservation laws of momentum and total energy are expressed by

\displaystyle\xi+\xi_{*}=\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{*}^{\prime}\,,\ \tfrac{1}{2}|\xi|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|\xi_{*}|^{2}+I+I_{*}=\tfrac{1}{2}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|\xi_{*}^{\prime}|^{2}+I^{\prime}+I_{*}^{\prime}\,,

where
(I,I_{*})
and
(I^{\prime},I_{*}^{\prime})
are respectively the pre-internal energy and post-internal energy variables. In the corresponding cross section, the four “points”
(\xi,\xi_{*},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{*}^{\prime})
only form a parallelogram (not a rectangle), see Figure
2
.

\xi_{*}^{\prime}
\xi^{\prime}
\xi
\xi_{*}

Figure 2.
Transformation of velocities for polyatomic molecules with same mass.

Moreover, if the two collided polyatomic molecules are of different masses, the four “points”
(\xi,\xi_{*},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{*}^{\prime})
in the collisional cross section will only form a trapezoid, see Figure 1 of
[
12
]
for example. From the geometric intuitivity above, the structure of the Boltzmann collision integral for the polyatomic gases is more complex than that for the monatomic gases.

Very recently, Bernhoff
[
12
]
wrote down a more complicated rarefied gases model captured a multicomponent mixture of
s
species
a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{s}
with
s_{0}
monatomic and
s_{1}:=s-s_{0}
polyatomic species, and masses
m_{1},m_{2},\cdots,m_{s}
, respectively. This model involves the classical Boltzmann collision effect for monatomic molecules, the complex Boltzmann collision interaction possessing continuous internal energy variable
I\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
for polyatomic molecules, and also the interaction collisional effect between monatomic and polyatomic molecules. More precisely, the model what we consider in current work is formulated by the following vector-valued Boltzmann equation

(1.1)

\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f=Q(f,f),

where
f=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
is the density distribution function with the components
f_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z})
for
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
at
t>0
, position
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
associated with mixed microscopic variable
\mathbf{Z}
given by

(1.2)

\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}:=\begin{cases}\xi,\qquad\kern 5.0pt\,\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}\,,\\
(\xi,I),\quad\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}\,.\end{cases}

Here
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the average velocity variable of the microscopic particles and
I>0
is the continuous internal energy variable. Namely,
f_{\alpha}(t,x,\xi)
(
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
) represent the density distribution of the monatomic molecules, and
f_{\alpha}(t,x,\xi,I)
(
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
) capture that of the polyatomic molecules.

Moreover, the vector-valued collision operator
Q=(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{s})
is a quadratic bilinear operator expressing the change of the velocities and internal energies of particles due to binary collisions. Here
Q_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
is the collision operator for species
a_{\alpha}
, the detailed expression of which will be given later. Then the components forms of (
1.1
) can be represented as

(1.3)

\tfrac{\partial f_{\alpha}}{\partial t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{\alpha}=Q_{\alpha}(f,f)\,,\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}\,.

A collision between two particles of species
a_{\alpha}
and
a_{\beta}
with
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
can be respectively expressed by pre-collisional elements
(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}_{*})=(\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha},\mathbf{Z}_{*\beta})
and post-collisional elements
(\mathbf{Z}^{\prime},\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}^{\prime},\mathbf{Z}_{*\beta}^{\prime})
, where the all collisional elements consist of a microscopic velocity and possibly an internal energy. The notations for pre- and post-collisional pairs may be interchanged as well. Taking the momentum and total energy conservation into consideration, the following relations hold:

(1)

Momentum conservation laws: for
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s\}

(1.4)

m_{\alpha}\mathbf{\xi}+m_{\beta}\mathbf{\xi}_{*}=m_{\alpha}\mathbf{\xi}^{\prime}+m_{\beta}\mathbf{\xi}^{\prime}_{*}\,;

(2)

Total energy conservation laws:

•

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

(1.5)

m_{\alpha}|\mathbf{\xi}|^{2}+m_{\beta}|\xi_{*}|^{2}=m_{\alpha}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+m_{\beta}|\xi^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}\,;

•

If
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and
\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.6)

\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi_{*}|^{2}+I_{*}=\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}+I^{\prime}_{*}\,;

•

If
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
and
\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

(1.7)

\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi_{*}|^{2}+I=\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}+I^{\prime}\,;

•

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.8)

\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi_{*}|^{2}+I+I_{*}=\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}+I^{\prime}+I^{\prime}_{*}\,.

Remark that the above energy conservation laws can be written as the following unified form

(1.9)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi_{*}|^{2}+\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I+\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\alpha}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\beta}|\xi^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}+\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I^{\prime}+\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I^{\prime}_{*}\,.

1.2.
Collision operator and its equivalent transformation

1.2.1.
Definition of collision operator
Q_{\alpha}

For
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
, as in
[
12
]
, the component collision operator
Q_{\alpha}(f,f)
for species
a_{\alpha}
can be introduced by

(1.10)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}(f,f)=\sum\limits_{\beta=1}^{s}Q_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=\sum\limits_{\beta=1}^{s}\int_{\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}}W_{\alpha\beta}\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}(f)d\mathbf{Z}_{*}d\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}d\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\prime}\,,

where

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\gamma}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathbb{R}^{3}\,,&\textrm{ if }\gamma\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}\,,\\
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\,,&\textrm{ if }\gamma\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}\,,\end{aligned}\right.

and

(1.12)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}(f):=\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}\,.

Here
\delta^{(\alpha)}\geq 2
(
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
) with
\delta^{(1)}=\cdots=\delta^{(s_{0})}=2
are the indices of the degeneracies, see Gamba and Pavic-Colic’s work
[
21
]
, which denote the number of internal degrees
of freedom of the monatomic or polyatomic species
a_{\alpha}
. Here and below the abbreviations
f_{\alpha_{*}}=f_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{*}),f^{\prime}_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}^{\prime})
and
f^{\prime}_{\alpha_{*}}=f_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{*})
are employed, where
\mathbf{Z}_{*}
,
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{*}
are defined as the same way of (
1.2
).

As in
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
, the transition probabilities
W_{\alpha\beta}
are introduced by

(1.13)

\displaystyle W_{\alpha\beta}=

\displaystyle W_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}_{*}|\mathbf{Z}^{\prime},\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta})^{2}m_{\alpha}m_{\beta}(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\sigma^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}\tfrac{|g^{\prime}|}{|g|}\hat{\delta}_{1}\hat{\delta}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta})^{2}m_{\alpha}m_{\beta}I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\sigma_{\alpha\beta}\tfrac{|g|}{|g^{\prime}|}\hat{\delta}_{1}\hat{\delta}_{3}\,,

where

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}_{1}=\delta_{1}(\tfrac{1}{2}(m_{\alpha}|\xi|^{2}+m_{\beta}|\xi_{*}|^{2}-m_{\alpha}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}-m_{\beta}|\xi^{\prime}_{*}|^{2})-\Delta I)\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}_{3}=\delta_{3}(m_{\alpha}\xi+m_{\beta}\xi_{*}-m_{\alpha}\xi^{\prime}-m_{\beta}\xi^{\prime}_{*})\,,

\displaystyle\cos\theta=\tfrac{g\cdot g^{\prime}}{|g||g^{\prime}|}\,,g=\xi-\xi_{*},g^{\prime}=\xi^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}_{*}\,.

Here
\delta_{3}
and
\delta_{1}
denote the Dirac’s delta function in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbb{R}
, respectively. Moreover, the scattering cross sections
\sigma_{\alpha\beta}
,
\sigma_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime}>0
and the energy gap
\Delta I\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy the following properties:

(1)

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|)\,,\sigma^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g^{\prime}|,|\cos\theta|)\,,\Delta I=0\,;

(2)

If
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and
\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I_{*},I_{*}^{\prime})\,,\sigma^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g^{\prime}|,|\cos\theta|,I_{*}^{\prime},I_{*})\,,\Delta I=I_{*}^{\prime}-I_{*}\,;

(3)

If
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
and
\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I,I^{\prime})\,,\sigma^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g^{\prime}|,|\cos\theta|,I^{\prime},I)\,,\Delta I=I^{\prime}-I\,;

(4)

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I,I_{*},I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{*})\,,\sigma^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g^{\prime}|,|\cos\theta|,I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{*},I,I_{*})\,,

\displaystyle\Delta I=I^{\prime}+I^{\prime}_{*}-I-I_{*}\,.

Here
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
represents the velocities deflection angular between pre- and post-collisions. By summarizing the above relations, the internal energy gap
\Delta I
can be unified as

\displaystyle\Delta I=\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I^{\prime}+\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I_{*}^{\prime}-\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I-\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I_{*}\,.

Moreover, the scattering cross sections
\sigma_{\alpha\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
are assumed to satisfy the microreversibility conditions

(1.14)

(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}|g^{\prime}|^{2}\sigma^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}|g|^{2}\sigma_{\alpha\beta}

and the symmetry relations

(1.15)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(

\displaystyle|g|,|\cos\theta|,I,I_{*},I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{*})=\sigma_{\beta\alpha}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I_{*},I,I^{\prime}_{*},I^{\prime})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\alpha}=

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\alpha}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I,I_{*},I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{*})=\sigma_{\alpha\alpha}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I_{*},I,I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\alpha}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I_{*},I,I^{\prime}_{*},I^{\prime})\,.

Moreover, we shall adopt in this paper an assumption of weak angular cut-off (see Grad
[
27
]
), namely,
\sigma_{\alpha\beta}
are integrable associated with the angular variable
\theta
, i.e.,

(1.16)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{Z}^{\prime},\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\prime},\theta)\in L^{1}_{loc}(d\mathbf{Z}_{*};L^{1}(d\theta))\,,

where the variables
\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{Z}^{\prime},\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\prime}
subject to the conservation laws (
1.4
)-(
1.9
), and the deflection angular
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
. As a result, together with the above relations and the known properties of Dirac’s delta function, the transition probabilities
W_{\alpha\beta}
can be transformed to

\displaystyle W_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\mu_{\alpha\beta}\ \sigma^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}\tfrac{|g^{\prime}|}{|g|}1_{\mu_{\alpha\beta}|g|^{2}>2\Delta I}\delta_{3}(G_{\alpha\beta}-G^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta})\delta_{1}(\sqrt{|g|^{2}-\tfrac{2\Delta I}{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}}-|g^{\prime}|)

\displaystyle=I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\mu_{\alpha\beta}\ \sigma_{\alpha\beta}\tfrac{|g|}{|g^{\prime}|}1_{\mu_{\alpha\beta}|g|^{2}>2\Delta I}\delta_{3}(G_{\alpha\beta}-G^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta})\delta_{1}(\sqrt{|g|^{2}-\tfrac{2\Delta I}{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}}-|g^{\prime}|)\,,

where

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}=\tfrac{m_{\alpha}\mathbf{\xi}+m_{\beta}\mathbf{\xi}_{*}}{m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta}}\,,G^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=\tfrac{m_{\alpha}\mathbf{\xi^{\prime}}+m_{\beta}\mathbf{\xi^{\prime}}_{*}}{m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta}}\,,\mu_{\alpha\beta}=\tfrac{m_{\alpha}m_{\beta}}{(m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta})}\,.

Remark that the collision operator
Q_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)
can be split into the gain term
Q^{+}_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)
and loss term
Q^{-}_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)
, namely,

Q_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=Q^{+}_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)-Q^{-}_{\alpha\beta}(f,f),

where

\displaystyle Q^{+}_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}}W_{\alpha\beta}\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}d\mathbf{Z}_{*}d\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}d\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\prime},

\displaystyle Q^{-}_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\beta}}W_{\alpha\beta}\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}d\mathbf{Z}_{*}d\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}d\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\prime}.

1.2.2.
Equivalent transformation of the collision operator

Notice that the total molecular energy conservation law from (
1.9
) can be simply written as

(1.17)

\displaystyle E_{\alpha\beta}=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}{2}|g|^{2}+\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I+\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}{2}|g^{\prime}|^{2}+\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I^{\prime}+\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I_{*}^{\prime}.

If
\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
, one introduces a parameter
\mathfrak{R}\in[0,1]
such that

(1.18)

\mathfrak{R}E_{\alpha\beta}=\tfrac{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}{2}|g^{\prime}|^{2},\quad(1-\mathfrak{R})E_{\alpha\beta}=\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I^{\prime}+I^{\prime}_{*}.

Moreover, if
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
, one introduces a parameter
\mathfrak{r}\in[0,1]
such that

(1.19)

I^{\prime}=\mathfrak{r}(1-\mathfrak{R})E_{\alpha\beta},\quad I^{\prime}_{*}=(1-\mathfrak{r})(1-\mathfrak{R})E_{\alpha\beta}.

Finally, for the sake of symmetry,we introduce the extra parameters

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}=\tfrac{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}{2E_{\alpha\beta}}|g|^{2},\quad\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=\tfrac{I}{I+I_{*}}.

By a series of change of variables
[
12
]
, there holds

\displaystyle d\xi^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}_{*}dI^{\prime}dI^{\prime}_{*}=

\displaystyle dG^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}dg^{\prime}dI^{\prime}dI^{\prime}_{*}=\ |g^{\prime}|^{2}dG^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}d|g^{\prime}|d\omega dI^{\prime}dI^{\prime}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\tfrac{E_{\alpha\beta}}{\mu_{\alpha\beta}})^{3/2}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega dG^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}dI^{\prime}dE^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\sqrt{2}}{\mu_{\alpha\beta}^{3/2}}E_{\alpha\beta}^{5/2}(1-\mathfrak{R})\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d\mathfrak{r}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega dG^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}dE^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}.

Then the collision operators
Q_{\alpha\beta}
can be transformed to the following forms.

(1)

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

(1.20)

Q_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},\omega)(f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}-f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}})d\xi_{*}d\omega,

where

(1.21)

\displaystyle B_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},\omega)=\tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}|g|,\quad\tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,\omega)=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|)\,;

(2)

If
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and
\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.22)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=

\displaystyle\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\omega)(\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})

\displaystyle\times(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}d\xi_{*}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega dI_{*},

where

(1.23)

\displaystyle B_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\omega)=\tfrac{\tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}|g|E_{\alpha\beta}}{\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}},\ \tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\omega)=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I_{*},I^{\prime}_{*})\,;

(3)

If
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
and
\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

(1.24)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{*},I,\mathfrak{R},\omega)(\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}}-\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}})

\displaystyle\times(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}d\xi_{*}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega\,,

where

(1.25)

\displaystyle B_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{*},I,\mathfrak{R},\omega)=\tfrac{\tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}|g|E_{\alpha\beta}}{\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}},\ \tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,I,\mathfrak{R},\omega)=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I,I^{\prime})\,;

(4)

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.26)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}(f,f)=

\displaystyle\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,1]^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})

\displaystyle\times B_{2\alpha\beta}\ \mathfrak{r}^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(1-\mathfrak{r})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}(1-\mathfrak{R})^{(\delta(\alpha)+\delta(\beta))/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}d\xi_{*}d\mathfrak{r}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega dI_{*}\,,

where

(1.27)

\displaystyle B_{2\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I+I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{r},\omega)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}|g|E^{2}_{\alpha\beta}}{\mathfrak{r}^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(1-\mathfrak{r})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}(1-\mathfrak{R})^{(\delta(\alpha)+\delta(\beta))/2-2}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,I+I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{r},\omega)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\alpha\beta}(|g|,|\cos\theta|,I+I_{*},I^{\prime}+I^{\prime}_{*})

In the above collision integrals, the post-velocities
(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{*}^{\prime})
can be represented by

(1.28)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \xi^{\prime}=&\tfrac{m_{\alpha}\xi+m_{\beta}\xi_{*}}{m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta}}+\omega\tfrac{m_{\beta}}{m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta}}\sqrt{|g|^{2}-\tfrac{2\Delta I}{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}}\,,\\
\xi_{*}^{\prime}=&\tfrac{m_{\alpha}\xi+m_{\beta}\xi_{*}}{m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta}}-\omega\tfrac{m_{\alpha}}{m_{\alpha}+m_{\beta}}\sqrt{|g|^{2}-\tfrac{2\Delta I}{\mu_{\alpha\beta}}}\end{aligned}\right.

with parameter
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Together with the symmetric properties in (
1.14
)-(
1.15
), the new forms of the collision integral kernels
B_{i\alpha\beta}
(
i=0,1,2
) defined in (
1.21
), (
1.23
) and (
1.27
) are invariant under the following change of variables:

(1.29)

\displaystyle(\xi,\xi_{*},I,I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{r},\omega)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}_{*},I^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{*},\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(\xi,\xi_{*},I,I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{r},\omega)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow(\xi_{*},\xi,I_{*},I,\mathfrak{R},1-\mathfrak{r},-\omega).

Namely,

•

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

(1.30)

B_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},\omega)=B_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}_{*},\omega^{\prime})=B_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi_{*}-\xi,-\omega)\,;

•

If
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and
\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.31)

B_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\omega)=B_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}_{*},I^{\prime}_{*},\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})=B_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi_{*}-\xi,I,\mathfrak{R},-\omega)\,;

•

If
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
and
\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

(1.32)

B_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{*},I,\mathfrak{R},\omega)=B_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}_{*},I^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})=B_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi_{*}-\xi,I,\mathfrak{R},-\omega).\,;

•

If
\alpha,\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.33)

\displaystyle B_{2\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I+I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{r},\omega)

\displaystyle=B_{2\alpha\beta}(\xi^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}_{*},I^{\prime}+I^{\prime}_{*},\mathfrak{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})

\displaystyle=B_{2\alpha\beta}(\xi_{*}-\xi,I_{*}+I,\mathfrak{R},1-\mathfrak{r},-\omega)\,.

Together with the angular cutoff assumptions (
1.16
), one can further define the following functions

(1.34)

\displaystyle A_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},\omega)d\omega,

\displaystyle A_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I_{*})=

\displaystyle\iint_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\omega)(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega,

\displaystyle A_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{*},I)=

\displaystyle\iint_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{*},I,\mathfrak{R},\omega)(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega,

\displaystyle A_{2\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I+I_{*})=

\displaystyle\iiint_{[0,1]^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{2\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I+I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{r},\omega)

\displaystyle\times\mathfrak{r}^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(1-\mathfrak{r})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}(1-\mathfrak{R})^{(\delta(\alpha)+\delta(\beta))/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d\mathfrak{r}d\mathfrak{R}d\omega.

Employing the above kernel functions, one introduces the following linear operators

\displaystyle L_{0\alpha\beta}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\beta_{*}}A_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*})d\xi_{*},\ L_{1\alpha\beta}(f)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\beta_{*}}A_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I_{*})d\xi_{*}dI_{*},

(1.35)

\displaystyle L_{1\beta\alpha}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\beta_{*}}A_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{*},I)d\xi_{*},\ L_{2\alpha\beta}(f)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\beta_{*}}A_{2\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I+I_{*})d\xi_{*}dI_{*}\,.

Actually, the above definitions can represent the loss term
Q_{\alpha\beta}^{-}(f,f)
as follows:

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}^{-}(f,f)=f_{\alpha}L_{0\alpha\beta}(f)\,,\quad\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}^{-}(f,f)=f_{\alpha}L_{1\alpha\beta}(f)\,,\quad\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}\,,\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}^{-}(f,f)=f_{\alpha}L_{1\beta\alpha}(f)\,,\quad\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}\,,\beta\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}\,,

(1.36)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha\beta}^{-}(f,f)=f_{\alpha}L_{2\alpha\beta}(f)\,,\quad\alpha,\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}\,.

Furthermore, we also introduce

(1.37)

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}(f)=\sum_{\beta=1}^{s_{0}}L_{0\alpha\beta}(f)+\sum_{\beta=s_{0}+1}^{s}L_{1\alpha\beta}(f)\,,\quad\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}\,,

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}(f)=\sum_{\beta=1}^{s_{0}}L_{1\beta\alpha}(f)+\sum_{\beta=s_{0}+1}^{s}L_{2\alpha\beta}(f)\,,\quad\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}\,,

which implies

(1.38)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}^{-}(f,f)=\sum_{\beta=1}^{s}Q_{\alpha\beta}^{-}(f,f)=f_{\alpha}L_{\alpha}(f)\,,\quad\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}\,.

1.3.
Notations and main results

1.3.1.
Notations

In this paper, the symbol
\mathbb{R}^{d}
means the d-dimensional real Euclidean space for integer
d\geq 1
.
\mathbb{R}_{+}
stands for the positive half real line, i.e.,
\mathbb{R}_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1};x>0\}
.
\mathbb{S}^{2}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3};|x|=1\}
is the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
\mathbb{N}
denotes by the nonnegative integers set, and
\mathbb{N}^{k}=\{\boldsymbol{n}=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{k})|n_{i}\in\mathbb{N},1\leq i\leq k\}
for any integer
k\geq 1
. For any
\boldsymbol{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we define its
1
-norm
|\boldsymbol{n}|=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
. The brief notation “a.e.” means “almost everywhere”.
L^{p}(d\mu)
represents the standard
L^{p}
space associated with the measure
\mu
. For any functional space
F
, the symbol
F_{+}
stands for
\{f\in F;f\geq 0\textrm{ a.e.}\}
. Moreover,
B_{R}
is the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
centered at
0
with radius
R\in(0,\infty)
.

We also consider the real Hilbert space

X:=(L^{2}(d\mathbf{\xi}))^{s_{0}}\times(L^{2}(d\mathbf{\xi}dI))^{s_{1}},

with inner product

(1.39)

(f,g)=\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{s_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}g_{\alpha}d\xi+\sum\limits_{\alpha=s_{0}+1}^{s}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}g_{\alpha}d\xi dI,\ f,g\in X.

For any smooth function
f(t,x,\xi)
over
(t,x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{6})\in\mathbb{N}^{7}
, we define the partial derivative operator
D^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
as

\displaystyle D^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}f=\frac{\partial^{|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|}f}{\partial t^{\zeta_{0}}\partial x_{1}^{\zeta_{1}}\partial x_{2}^{\zeta_{2}}\partial x_{3}^{\zeta_{3}}\partial\xi_{1}^{\zeta_{4}}\partial\xi_{2}^{\zeta_{5}}\partial\xi_{3}^{\zeta_{6}}}\,.

Moreover, for any smooth function
g(t,x,\xi,I)
over
(t,x,\xi,I)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{6},\gamma_{7})\in\mathbb{N}^{8}
, we introduce the partial derivative operator
D^{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
as

\displaystyle D^{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}g=\frac{\partial^{|\boldsymbol{\gamma}|}g}{\partial t^{\gamma_{0}}\partial x_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\partial x_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}\partial x_{3}^{\gamma_{3}}\partial\xi_{1}^{\gamma_{4}}\partial\xi_{2}^{\gamma_{5}}\partial\xi_{3}^{\gamma_{6}}\partial I^{\gamma_{7}}}\,.

In this paper, all limits
\lim\limits_{n\to+\infty}f^{n}
are in the sense of subsequence if necessary.

1.3.2.
Initial data

In this paper, we focus on the Cauchy problem of the equation (
1.1
), whose initial data is

(1.40)

f(t,x,\mathbf{Z})|_{t=0}=f_{0}(x,\mathbf{Z})=(f_{1,0},\cdots,f_{s,0})(x,\mathbf{Z})

with the following assumptions:

(1)

For all
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.41)

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,0}\geq 0\quad a.e.\ (x,\mathbf{Z})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{Z_{\alpha}}\,,

where
\mathcal{Z_{\alpha}}
is given in (
1.11
);

(2)

The components
f_{\alpha,0}
of the initial data
f_{0}
satisfy:

•

If
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,

(1.42)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha,0}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+|\log f_{\alpha,0}|)dxd\xi<\infty\,.

•

If
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,

(1.43)

\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha,0}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I+|\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha,0})|)dxd\xi dI<\infty.

1.3.3.
Hypotheses on collision kernels

Based on the angular cutoff assumption (
1.16
), we have introduced the average collision kernel functions
A_{i\alpha\beta}
(
i=0,1,2
) and
A_{1\beta\alpha}
in (
1.34
). The following hypotheses about
A_{i\alpha\beta}
(
i=0,1,2
) and
A_{1\beta\alpha}
will be imposed: for any fixed
0<R<+\infty
,

(1.44)

\displaystyle(1+|\xi|^{2})^{-1}\int_{|z-\xi|\leq R}A_{0\alpha\beta}(z)dz\rightarrow 0,\quad|\xi|\rightarrow\infty,

\displaystyle(1+|\xi|^{2}+\eta)^{-1}\int_{|z-\xi|\leq R}A_{1\alpha\beta}(z,\eta)dz\rightarrow 0,\quad|\xi|,\eta\rightarrow\infty,

\displaystyle(1+|\xi|^{2})^{-1}\int_{|z-\xi|\leq R}\int_{0<\eta<R}A_{1\beta\alpha}(z,\eta)dzd\eta\rightarrow 0,\quad|\xi|\rightarrow\infty,

\displaystyle(1+|\xi|^{2}+I)^{-1}\int_{|z-\xi|\leq R}\int_{0<\eta-I<R}A_{2\alpha\beta}(z,\eta)dzd\eta\rightarrow 0,\quad|\xi|,I\rightarrow\infty.

1.3.4.
Definition of renormalized solutions

Definition 1.1
.

We say that
f=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
is a renormalized solution of (
1.1
) if it satisfies

(1)

For
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,
g_{\alpha}=\beta(f_{\alpha})=\log(1+f_{\alpha})
solves

\tfrac{\partial g_{\alpha}}{\partial t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q_{\alpha}(f,f)\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}),

and
\frac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q_{\alpha}^{\pm}(f,f)\in L_{loc}^{1}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
;

(2)

For
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,
g_{\alpha}
solves

\tfrac{\partial g_{\alpha}}{\partial t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}g_{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q_{\alpha}(f,f)\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}),

and
\frac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q_{\alpha}^{\pm}(f,f)\in L_{loc}^{1}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

Remark 1.1
.

As shown in Lemma
2.8
later, the definition of the renormalized solution above is equivalent to that for all
\beta(\cdot)\in C^{1}[0,+\infty)
such that
|\beta^{\prime}(t)|\leq\tfrac{C}{1+t}
,

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial\beta(f_{\alpha})}{\partial t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta(f_{\alpha})=\beta^{\prime}(f_{\alpha})Q_{\alpha}(f,f)\ (\alpha\in\{1,\ \cdots,s\})

hold in the sense of distribution.

Next we introduce Boltzmann-type H-functional by

(1.45)

\displaystyle H(f)(t)

\displaystyle=(f,\log(\varphi^{-1}f))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{s_{0}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}\log f_{\alpha}dxd\xi+\sum\limits_{\alpha=s_{0}+1}^{s}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})dxd\xi dI.

Now we state the main result of current work.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that the collision kernels satisfy (
1.16
) and (
1.44
). Let the initial data
f_{0}=(f_{1,0},\cdots,f_{s,0})
obeys (
1.41
), (
1.42
) and (
1.43
). Then the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.40
) admits a renormalized solution
f=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
enjoying the following properties: For any given
T<\infty
and
0<R<\infty
, there is a
C_{T}>0
such that

(1)

for
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\},f_{\alpha}\in C([0,\infty);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}))_{+},

(1.46)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q^{-}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times B_{R})),\ \tfrac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q^{+}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times B_{R}),

(1.47)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+|\log f_{\alpha}|)dxd\xi\leq C_{T};

(2)

for
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\},f_{\alpha}\in C([0,\infty);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}))_{+},

(1.48)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q^{-}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times B_{R}\times(0,R))),

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q^{+}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times B_{R}\times(0,R)),

(1.49)

\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I+|\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})|)dxd\xi dI<C_{T};

(3)

The following entropy inequality holds:

(1.50)

\displaystyle H(f)(t)+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{s_{0}}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}e_{\alpha}(\tau,x,\xi)dxd\xi

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum\limits_{\alpha=s_{0}+1}^{s}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}e_{\alpha}(\tau,x,\xi,I)dxd\xi dI\leq H(f_{0}),

where

(1.51)

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\beta=1}^{s}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})^{3}}(\tfrac{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-1)

\displaystyle\times\log(\tfrac{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}W_{\alpha\beta}d\xi_{*}d\xi^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}_{*}dI_{*}dI^{\prime}dI^{\prime}_{*}.

1.4.
Sketch of proofs

In this subsection, we mainly sketch the ideas of proving the main theorem. Since the Boltzmann collision operator
Q(f,f)
is not integrable, it is impossible to prove the weak solution of the problem (
1.1
). In 1989, DiPerna-Lions
[
16
]
introduced the so-called renormalized solution to the transported equations. Then they also defined the renormalized solution to the classical Boltzmann equation and proved its global existence with large initial data in
[
17
]
. Inspired by their works, we first give the definition of the renormalized solution to the Boltzmann system (
1.1
) in Definition
1.1
, in which the factor
\frac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}
in the
f_{\alpha}
-equation is such that
\frac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q_{\alpha}(f,f)
is locally integrable. Thus the weak solutions of the renormalized form can be introduced. We remark that the equivalent expressions of renormalized solution are given in Lemma
2.8
. The main goal of current paper is to verify the global existence of the renormalized solution with large initial data.

Step 1. Construction of smooth approximation problem (
3.1
).
While constructing the smoothly approximated problem (
3.1
) of the original problem (
1.1
), there are four key ingredients: 1) smooth approximation of initial data; 2) smooth approximation of collision kernels; 3) existence, uniqueness and positivity of smooth approximated problem (
3.1
); 4) uniform bounds of the approximated problem in the a priori spaces.

We initially construct the smoothly approximated initial data
f^{n}_{0}=(f^{n}_{\alpha,0})_{\alpha\{1,\cdots,s\}}
in the Schwartz space with positive components as in Lemma
3.1
and
3.2
. The constructed approximated initial data subject to the integrability (
1.42
)-(
1.43
) uniformly in
n\geq 1
. Moreover, by using the weak lower semicontinuity of the entropy product
H(f)
given in Lemma
2.6
, the approximated initial data constructed above also converge to the initial data
f_{0}
in the sense of entropy product, see Lemma
3.3
.

We next construct the smoothly approximated collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
beginning with the collision kernels approximation. Note that the collision kernels
B_{2\alpha\beta}
,
B_{1\alpha\beta}
,
B_{1\beta\alpha}
and
B_{0\alpha\beta}
may possess the singularity at
|z|,\eta=0,\infty
and the deflection angular
\theta=0
, i.e.,
z\cdot\omega=0
. As in Lemma
3.4
-
3.5
-
3.6
, we take zero-approximation near the underlying singular points and use the smooth modifiers, so that the approximated collision kernels
B_{2\alpha\beta,n}
,
B_{1\alpha\beta,n}
,
B_{1\beta\alpha,n}
and
B_{0\alpha\beta,n}
pointwisely converge to the original collision kernels as
n\to+\infty
. Moreover, together with the angular cutoff assumption (
1.16
), the reduced collision kernels
A_{2\alpha\beta,n}
,
A_{1\alpha\beta,n}
,
A_{1\beta\alpha,n}
and
A_{0\alpha\beta,n}
obey the assumptions (
1.44
) uniformly in
n\geq 1
. Consequently, one can construct the approximated collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})=(\tilde{Q}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n}))_{\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}}
in (
3.16
). Remark that the factor
N_{n}(f^{n})^{-1}
given in (
3.17
) is such that the nonlinear approximated collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
admits linear upper bounds in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
as in Lemma
3.7
and
3.8
. Moreover, Lemma
3.7
shows that
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
is Lipschitz continuous
L^{1}
space, which will be used to prove the well-posedness of the approximated problem (
3.1
). On the
L^{1}
estimates, the
L^{1}
norm of the loss term
\tilde{Q}^{-}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})=f^{n}\tilde{L}_{n}(f^{n})
can easily be bounded by the
L^{1}
norm of
f^{n}
due to
\tilde{L}_{n}(f^{n})\in L^{\infty}
. In order to estimate the
L^{1}
norm of the gain term
\tilde{Q}^{+}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
, we shall utilize the relation
\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}Q^{+}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f,f)\varphi d\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}Q^{-}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f,f)\varphi^{\prime}d\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}
to shift the gain term to the loss term, so that the gain term can be bounded by using the same arguments of the loss term. About the
L^{\infty}
estimates, the
L^{\infty}
norm of the loss term
\tilde{Q}^{-}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
can be obtained by the analogous arguments of
L^{1}
norm of loss term. For the gain term, the key is to obtain the bound (
3.20
), in which the quantity
I_{2\alpha\beta,n}
is dominated by (
3.22
). For the sake of controlling
I_{2\alpha\beta,n}
by
L^{1}
norm of
f^{n}_{\beta}
, we should discuss the internal energy gap
\Delta I=\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I^{\prime}+\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I_{*}^{\prime}-\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I-\mathbf{1}_{\beta\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}}I_{*}
by the cases
\Delta I\leq 0
and
\Delta I>0
as in Lemma
3.8
. Together with
N_{n}(f^{n})^{-1}
, the
L^{\infty}
norm of the gain term can be bounded by that of
f^{n}
.

We then prove the well-posedness, positivity and regularity of the approximated problem (
3.1
). Together with Lemma
3.7
, the operator semigroup theory shows the existence and uniqueness of (
3.1
) in
C([0,+\infty);\prod_{\alpha=1}^{s}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha};dxd\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}))
. For the positivity, we first consider the auxiliary Cauchy problem (
3.29
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}g^{n}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}g^{n}=F(g^{n})\,,\ g^{n}|_{t=0}=f_{0}\,.

Following the same arguments of well-posedness of (
3.1
), the above auxiliary problem for
g^{n}
is also well-posed in
C([0,+\infty);\prod_{\alpha=1}^{s}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha};dxd\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}))
. Then mild form of
g^{n}
shows that the components of
g^{n}
are nonnegative under the positive components of initial data
f_{0}
. As a result, the
g^{n}
equation is exactly the approximated
f^{n}
equation. Thus, the Grönwall inequality for the approximated
f^{n}
equation can prove the positive lower bounds of the components of
f^{n}
. Furthermore, as in Lemma
3.10
, the mild form (
3.30
) of
f^{n}_{\alpha}
and Lemma
3.7
-
3.8
can imply the regularity of the approximated solution
f^{n}
by employing the Induction Principle for the orders of derivatives.

We finally derive the uniform bounds of the approximated solution
f^{n}
as in Lemma
3.11
. Note that the collision kernels
B_{2\alpha\beta,n}
,
B_{1\alpha\beta,n}
,
B_{1\beta\alpha,n}
and
B_{0\alpha\beta,n}
constructed in Lemmas
3.4
-
3.5
-
3.6
enjoy the same symmetry properties of
B_{2\alpha\beta}
,
B_{1\alpha\beta}
,
B_{1\beta\alpha}
and
B_{0\alpha\beta}
in (
1.21
)-(
1.23
)-(
1.25
)-(
1.27
). Then the all structural properties of the collision operator
Q
will also be valid for the approximated collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}=(\tilde{Q}_{\alpha,n})_{\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}}
constructed in (
3.16
). Then the collision invariant form of
Q

\varphi_{i}=ae_{i}+b\mathbf{m}\xi+c(\mathbf{m}|\xi|^{2}+2\mathbf{I}),\quad i=1,\cdots,s

is also that of
\tilde{Q}_{n}
, where
a,c\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
are arbitrary, see Proposition
2.2
. Consequently, one can easily obtain the required uniform bounds.

Step 2. Convergence of nonlinear approximated collision operator.
By the uniform bounds given in Lemma
3.11
and the sufficient conditions of relatively weak
L^{1}
compactness in Lemma
2.3
and Corollary
2.1
, we know that every component
f^{n}_{\alpha}
of
f^{n}
is weakly convergent to a nonnegative function
f_{\alpha}
in
L^{1}
. The difficulty is to prove the convergence of the nonlinear collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
.

First, we prove the weak
L^{1}
compactness of the nonlinear collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
. Note that the loss term
\tilde{Q}_{\alpha,n}^{-}(f^{n},f^{n})=f^{n}_{\alpha}\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})
has simpler structure than the gain term
\tilde{Q}_{\alpha,n}^{+}(f^{n},f^{n})
, so that we initially study the weak compactness of the loss term as in Lemma
4.1
. Due to
0\leq\frac{1}{1+f^{n}_{\alpha}}\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})\leq L_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})=\sum_{\beta=1}^{s}L_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})
, we only need to show the relatively weak compactness of
L_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})
in
L^{1}
. Instead, we consider the cutoff operator
L^{m}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}A_{\alpha\beta,n}(\xi-\xi_{*},I+I_{*})\mathbf{1}_{|\xi-\xi_{*}|\leq m}\mathbf{1}_{I+I_{*}<m}f^{n}_{\beta*}d\xi_{*}dI_{*}
for any fixed
m>0
. One can prove that
L^{m}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})
satisfy (
4.5
), i.e.,

\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times B_{R}\times(0,R)}\Psi(L^{m}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n}))(t)dxd\xi dI\leq C(T,m)

with
\Psi(t)=t(\log t)^{+}
. Then De La Vallée-Poussin Criterion (Lemma
2.2
) tells us that
L^{m}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})
is equi-integrable in
L^{1}
. The corresponding tightness is easy to be verified. The Dunford-Pettis Theorem (Lemma
2.1
) implies the relatively weak
L^{1}
compactness of
L^{m}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})
. Moreover, by using the assumptions (
1.44
), one gains that
L^{m}_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})
strongly converges to
L_{\alpha\beta,n}(f^{n})
as
m\to+\infty
, which finishes the proof of Lemma
4.1
. While proving the relatively weak
L^{1}
compactness of
\frac{1}{1+f^{n}_{\alpha}}\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})
as in Lemma
4.2
, one mainly needs to show the Arkeryd-type inequality

\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})\leq\tfrac{1}{\log K}\tilde{e}^{n}_{\alpha}+K\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})

for any fixed
K>1
. Then, together with Lemma
3.11
and Lemma
4.1
, the conclusions in Lemma
4.2
can be obtained.

Second, we study the properties of limits of
f^{n}
. The key is to establish the averaged velocity (-internal energy) Lemma
5.1
-
5.2
-
5.3
-
5.4
, whose proofs come from the works
[
17
,
23
,
24
]
. By now, we do not prove that the approximated solution
f^{n}
satisfies the conditions in the averaged velocity (-internal energy) lemmas. However, by Lemma
4.1
-
4.2
, the renormalized form
g^{n}_{\delta\alpha}=\frac{1}{\delta}\log(1+\delta f^{n}_{\alpha})
of
f^{n}_{\alpha}
exactly subjects to the required conditions, so that
\int g^{n}_{\delta\alpha}\varphi
strongly converges to
\int\tilde{g}_{\delta\alpha}\varphi
in
L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Fortunately, the strongly convergent relations between
g^{n}_{\delta\alpha}
and
f^{n}_{\alpha}
are established in Lemma
5.5
. Then the enhanced averaged velocity (-internal energy) lemma are derived in Lemma
5.6
and Corollary
5.1
, hence,
\int f^{n}_{\alpha}\varphi
strongly converges to
\int f_{\alpha}\varphi
in
L^{p}((0,T);L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
for
1\leq p<\infty
. Moreover, together Lemma
5.5
and the renormalized approximated solution
g^{n}_{\delta\alpha}
, as in Lemma
5.7
, we can prove that the limit function
f_{\alpha}\in C([0,\infty),L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}))_{+}
enjoys the bounds (
1.47
) and (
1.49
) in Theorem
1.1
. The core is to employ the mild form of
g^{n}_{\delta\alpha}
.

Third, we investigate the properties of limits of
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
. As stated before, the loss term
\tilde{Q}_{\alpha,n}^{-}(f^{n},f^{n})=f^{n}_{\alpha}\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})
admits a simpler structure than the gain term. So we initially consider the loss term whose main ingredient is the operator
\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})
. As in Lemma
5.8
, we can show that
\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})
strongly converges to
L_{\alpha}(f)
in
L^{1}
by applying the averaged velocity (-internal energy) lemmas and assumptions (
1.44
). Together with the enhanced averaged velocity (-internal energy) lemmas (Lemma
5.6
and Corollary
5.1
) and the Arkeryd-type inequality, Lemma
5.9
and Corollary
5.2
indicate that
\int\tilde{Q}^{\pm}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})\varphi\mathbf{1}_{E}
strongly converges to
\int Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)\varphi\mathbf{1}_{E}
in
L^{1}((0,T)\times B_{R})
for some finite measured Borel subset
E
, in which
\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})
is actually uniformly converges to
L_{\alpha}(f)
. Moreover, based on Lemma
5.9
and Corollary
5.2
, we can prove the following two convergent results: 1) For
\beta_{\delta}(t)=t\wedge\frac{1}{\delta}
or
\beta_{\delta}(t)=\frac{t}{1+\delta t}
,
\tilde{Q}^{\pm}_{\alpha,n}(\beta_{\delta}(f^{n}),\beta_{\delta}(f^{n}))
weakly converges to
Q^{\pm}(\tilde{\beta}_{\delta},\tilde{\beta}_{\delta})
in
L^{1}
, where w-
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\beta_{\delta}(f^{n})=\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}
(see Lemma
5.10
and Corollary
5.3
). 2) The sequence
\int\frac{\tilde{Q}^{\pm}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})}{1+\delta\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})}\varphi
strongly converges to
\int\frac{Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)}{1+\delta L_{\alpha}(f)}\varphi
in
L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
, see Lemma
5.11
and Corollary
5.4
. By employing the all above properties of limits, in Lemma
5.12
and Corollary
5.5
we can show that
\frac{Q_{\alpha}^{-}(f,f)}{1+f_{\alpha}}\in L^{\infty}_{t}L^{1}_{loc}
and
\frac{Q^{+}_{\alpha}(f,f)}{1+f_{\alpha}}\in L^{1}_{t}L^{1}_{loc}
, hence, the integrability (
1.46
) and (
1.48
) in Theorem
1.1
is valid.

Step 3. Existence of renormalized solution to (
1.1
).
As in Lemma
2.9
,
f
is a renormalized solution to (
1.1
) if and only if
f
is a mild solution with
\frac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}_{loc}
. Lemma
5.12
and Corollary
5.5
have been shown that
\frac{1}{1+f_{\alpha}}Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}_{loc}
. We thus only need to prove that the limit
f
is a mild solution. By Lemma
5.8
,
L_{\alpha}(f)\in L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times B_{R})
for
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and
L_{\alpha}(f)\in L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times B_{R}\times(0,R))
for
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
. Then Lemma
2.10
tells us that we only need to prove that
f
satisfies the identities (
2.8
) and (
2.9
), namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp}):=f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(s,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-(F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t)-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(s)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}Q^{+}_{\alpha}(f,f)^{\sharp}(s,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-(F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t)-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(\sigma)))d\sigma:=\mathcal{Q}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})\quad a.e.\ x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}.

We first prove the limit
f
is a supersolution to (
1.1
) given in Definition
6.1
, i.e.,
f
obeys the inequality
\mathcal{Q}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})\leq\mathcal{T}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})
. We start with the approximated identities (
6.1
)-(
6.2
), i.e.,

\displaystyle f^{n\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-f^{n}_{\alpha,0}(x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})^{\sharp}(s,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-(\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(s,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})))ds\,.

Lemma
6.1
easily implies that
f^{n\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-f^{n}_{\alpha,0}(x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}))
weakly converges to
\mathcal{T}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})
in
L^{1}
. However, there is no direct convergence on the gain term
\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})
(all results are associated with various constraints). We then consider
h^{n}_{M}=(h^{n}_{\alpha M})_{\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}}
with
h^{n}_{\alpha M}=f^{n}_{\alpha}\wedge M
, which means that
\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(h^{n}_{M},h^{n}_{M})\leq\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})
for
M>0
. Lemma
6.2
infers that
\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(h^{n}_{M},h^{n}_{M})^{\sharp}(s,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-(\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(s,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})))ds
weakly converges to
\mathcal{Q}(h_{\alpha M}^{\sharp})
in
L^{1}
by using Lemma
5.10
and Corollary
5.3
. Here
h_{\alpha M}
is the weak
L^{1}
limit of
h^{n}_{\alpha M}
. Then one has
\mathcal{Q}(h_{\alpha M}^{\sharp})\leq\mathcal{T}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})
. Moreover, (
6.7
) shows that
h_{\alpha M}
strongly converges to
f_{\alpha}
in
L^{1}
as
M\to+\infty
. It then implies that
\mathcal{Q}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})\leq\mathcal{T}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})
, namely,
f
is a suppersolution of (
1.1
), see Lemma
6.3
.

We second show the limit
f
is a subsolution to (
1.1
) given in Definition
6.2
, hence,
\mathcal{Q}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})\geq\mathcal{T}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})
. We mainly focus on the approximated renormalized equation (
5.1
), which can be equivalently expressed by (
6.10
), i.e.,

\displaystyle g^{n\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}(t)-g^{n}_{\delta\alpha,0}\mathrm{exp}(-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t))=\int_{0}^{t}\{\tfrac{\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})^{\sharp}}{1+\delta f^{n\sharp}_{\alpha}}+j^{n\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})^{\sharp}\}(\tau)\mathrm{exp}(-(\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t)-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(\tau)))d\tau,

where
j^{n}_{\delta\alpha}=g^{n}_{\delta\alpha}-\frac{f^{n}_{\alpha}}{1+\delta f^{n}_{\alpha}}
. By the similar arguments in Lemma
6.1
, one sees that
g^{n\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}(t)-g^{n}_{\delta\alpha,0}\mathrm{exp}(-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t))
weakly converges to
\tilde{g}^{\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}(t)-\tilde{g}_{\delta\alpha,0}\mathrm{exp}(-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t))
in
L^{1}
, where
\tilde{g}_{\delta\alpha}
is the weak limit of
g^{n}_{\delta\alpha}
. Lemma
6.4
then shows that
\int_{0}^{t}j^{n\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}\tilde{L}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n})^{\sharp}(\tau)\mathrm{exp}(-(\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(t)-\tilde{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha,n}(\tau)))d\tau
weakly converges to
\int_{0}^{t}j^{\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}L_{\alpha}(f)^{\sharp}(\tau)\mathrm{exp}(-({F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t)-{F}^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(\tau)))d\tau
in
L^{1}
, where
j_{\delta\alpha}
is the weak limit of
j^{n}_{\delta\alpha}
. Lemma
4.2
implies that
\tfrac{\tilde{Q}^{+}_{\alpha,n}(f^{n},f^{n})^{\sharp}}{1+\delta f^{n\sharp}_{\alpha}}
weakly converges to some function
Q^{+}_{\delta\alpha}
in
L^{1}
. Together with Lemma
5.9
and Corollary
5.2
, we can prove that
Q^{+}_{\delta\alpha}\leq Q^{+}_{\alpha}(f,f)
almost everywhere. As a result, one has

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-\tilde{g}_{\delta\alpha,0}(x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\{j^{\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}(\tau)L_{\alpha}(f)^{\sharp}(\tau)+Q^{+}_{\alpha}(f,f)^{\sharp}(\tau)\}\mathrm{exp}(-(F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t)-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(\tau)))d\tau\ a.e.

Lemma
5.5
tells us that
\tilde{g}^{\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-\tilde{g}_{\delta\alpha,0}(x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}))
pointwisely converges to
f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})-f_{\alpha,0}(x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha})\mathrm{exp}(-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}))
as
\delta\to 0^{+}
. Moreover, the relation (
6.22
) reads that
\int_{0}^{t}j^{\sharp}_{\delta\alpha}(\tau)L_{\alpha}(f)^{\sharp}(\tau)\mathrm{exp}(-(F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t)-F^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(\tau)))d\tau\to 0
pointwisely as
\delta\to 0^{+}
. Consequently,
\mathcal{Q}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})\geq\mathcal{T}(f_{\alpha}^{\sharp})
, hence,
f
is the subsolution to (
1.1
). Namely, we prove that
f
is the renormalized solution to (
1.1
).

Step 4. Entropy inequality.
In order to prove the entropy inequality (
1.50
) from the approximated entropy identity (
3.32
), it suffices to verify (
7.4
), i.e.,
\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\tilde{H}_{\alpha\beta}^{n}(f^{n})\geq H_{\alpha\beta}(f)
. We initially justify that the sequences
P^{n}_{\alpha\beta}=N_{n}(f^{n})^{-1}\frac{f^{n}_{\alpha}f^{n}_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}B^{R}_{2\alpha\beta,n}
and
\tilde{P}^{n}_{\alpha\beta}=N_{n}(f^{n})^{-1}\frac{{f_{\alpha}^{n}}^{\prime}{f_{\beta*}^{n}}^{\prime}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}B^{R}_{2\alpha\beta,n}
are relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta_{\alpha\beta})
for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times\in B_{R}
by employing the Dunford-Pettis Theorem (Lemma
2.1
). Following the analogous arguments of Lemmas
5.8
-
5.9
-
5.10
-
5.11
in Subsection
5.3
, we then can verify that
\int_{E_{R}}N(f^{n})^{-1}P^{n}_{\alpha\beta}\varphi d\Theta_{\alpha\beta}
and
\int_{E_{R}}N(f^{n})^{-1}\tilde{P}^{n}_{\alpha\beta}\varphi d\Theta_{\alpha\beta}
strongly converge to
\int_{E_{R}}N(f)^{-1}P_{\alpha\beta}\varphi d\Theta_{\alpha\beta}
and
\int_{E_{R}}N(f)^{-1}\tilde{P}_{\alpha\beta}\varphi d\Theta_{\alpha\beta}
, respectively, in
L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R})
, where
N(f)
is given in (
7.5
). Together with the fact
N(f^{n})
strongly converges to
N(f)
in
L^{1}((0,R)\times B_{R})
, we obtain that for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,R)\times B_{R}
,
P^{n}_{\alpha\beta}\rightarrow P_{\alpha\beta}
and
\tilde{P}^{n}_{\alpha\beta}\rightarrow\tilde{P}_{\alpha\beta}
weakly in
L^{1}(E_{R},d\Theta_{\alpha\beta})
. At the end, by using the convexity of the function

j(a,b)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}(a-b)\log\frac{a}{b},&\quad\text{for }a,b>0\,,\\
+\infty,&\quad\text{for }a\text{ or }b\leq 0\,,\end{array}\right.

there holds
\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{E_{R}}j(P^{n}_{\alpha\beta},\tilde{P}^{n}_{\alpha\beta})d\Theta_{\alpha\beta}\geq\int_{E_{R}}j(P_{\alpha\beta},\tilde{P}_{\alpha\beta})d\Theta_{\alpha\beta}
, which means that

\liminf\limits_{n\to+\infty}\tilde{H}_{\alpha\beta}^{n}(f^{n})\geq H_{\alpha\beta}(f)\,.

So the entropy inequality (
1.50
) in Theorem
1.1
is obtained.

1.5.
Historical remarks

Extensive research works have been carried out on the Boltzmann equation and related models, which can be classified into two main categories: 1) the classical solutions regime; 2) the renormalized solutions regime. In the framework of classical solutions, all works are imposed on the assumptions of the small initial data. For instance, the readers can be referred to the works
[
28
,
29
,
30
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
36
,
47
]
and the references therein. We here focus on the renormalized solutions regime.

We first review the existence of renormalized solutions for Boltzmann equation or related models. In 1989, DiPerna-Lions
[
16
]
introduced the so-called renormalized solution to the transported equations. Then they also defined the renormalized solution to the classical Boltzmann equation on angular cutoff model and proved its global existence over whole space with large initial data in
[
17
]
. In 1991, DiPerna-Lions
[
18
]
proved the entropy inequality of renormalized solution to the Boltzmann equation over whole space. In 1994, Villani
[
49
]
justified the existence of renormalized solution with a nonnegative measure to the Landau equation over whole spatial space. In 2002, Alexandre-Villani
[
2
]
verified the existence of renormalized solution with a nonnegative measure to the Boltzmann equation for angular noncutoff model over whole space. In 2010, Mischler
[
43
]
proved the existence of renormalized solution to the cutoff Boltzmann equation, Vlasov-Poisson and Fokker-Planck type models over bounded domain with Maxwell reflection boundary conditions. In 2019, Arsénio and Saint-Raymond
[
5
]
studied the renormalized solution to the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system over period domain. Moreover, in 2019, Jiang-Zhang investigated the renormalized solution of the Boltzmann equation for angular cutoff model in
[
37
]
and the renormalized solution with a nonnegative measure of the Boltzmann equation for angular noncutoff model in
[
38
]
over the bounded domain with incoming boundary condition.

We also remark that there were many works on the hydrodynamic limits in the renormalized solutions regime, which was initiated by Bardos-Golse-Levermore
[
6
,
7
]
in 1990s. Since then there has been many contributions to this program
[
8
,
22
,
25
,
26
,
31
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
45
]
. In particular, the work of Golse and Saint-Raymond
[
25
]
is the ﬁrst completed rigorous justiﬁcation of the NSF limit from the Boltzmann equation in a class of bounded collision kernels without making any nonlinear weak compactness hypotheses. They have recently extended their result to the case of hard potentials
[
26
]
. With some new nonlinear estimates, Levermore and Masmoudi
[
39
]
treated a broader class of collision kernels that includes all hard potential cases and, for the ﬁrst time in this program, soft potential cases. All works mentioned above were focused on either the periodic spatial domain or the whole space. For the bounded spatial domain issues, there also has been serval related works in the renormalized solutions regime. In
[
42
]
, Masmoudi and Saint-Raymond proved the linear Stokes limit from the fluctuations of renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation in a bounded domain. In
[
46
]
, Saint-Raymond rigorously justified the NSF limit in the renormalized solutions regime over a bounded domain. In
[
42
,
46
]
, the fluid boundary conditions were either the Dirichlet or the Navier slip boundary condition depending on the relative sizes of the accommodation coefﬁcient and the Knudsen number. In
[
35
]
, Jiang-Masmoudi rigorously proved the NSF limit with Neumann boundary condition from the renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation in a bounded domain with Maxwell reflection boundary condition for small accommodation coefficient
\sqrt{Kn}
. Moreover, Jiang-Zhang
[
37
,
38
]
rigorously verified the NSF limit from the renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation (including cutoff and noncutoff models) in a bounded domain with incoming boundary condition.

1.6.
Organization of current paper

In Section
2
, we give some preliminaries which will be frequently used in this paper. We construct the approximated problem (
3.1
) and establish its uniform bounds in Section
3
. The weak
L^{1}
compactness for nonlinear collision operator are given in Section
4
. Moreover, in Section
5
, we mainly study the convergence of the approximated solution
f^{n}
and the nonlinear collision operator
\tilde{Q}_{n}(f^{n},f^{n})
. The integrable properties of the corresponding limits are also studied in this section. Section
6
gives the proof of existence of renormalized solutions to (
1.1
). Section
7
gives the justification of the entropy inequality (
1.50
) in Theorem
1.1
. Finally, we verify the weak lower semicontinuity of entropy, i.e. prove Lemma
2.6
in Section
8
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Properties of weakly relatively compactness in
L^{1}

From the works
[
3
,
15
,
19
,
20
]
, we obtain the following properties.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be a subset of
L^{1}(X,\mathcal{M},\mu)
. We say that
\mathscr{F}
is equi-integrable if for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for any measurable set
E\subset X
with
\mu(E)<\delta
and for all
f\in\mathscr{F}
,

\int_{E}|f|dx<\epsilon.

It is a fact that
\mathscr{F}
being equi-integrable is equivalent to

\lim\limits_{C\rightarrow\infty}\sup\limits_{f\in\mathscr{F}}\int_{|f|>C}|f|d\mu=0.

Lemma 2.1

(Dunford-Pettis)
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{F}
is a Bounded subset of
L^{1}(X,\mathcal{M},\mu)
.
\mathscr{F}
is weakly relatively compact if and only if

(1)

\mathscr{F}
is equi-integrable;

(2)

for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists compact set
K\subset X
such that

\int_{K^{c}}|f(x)|dx<\epsilon,\quad\forall f\in\mathscr{F}.

Lemma 2.2

(De La Vallée-Poussin Criterion)
.

A subset
\mathscr{F}
of
L^{1}(X,\mathcal{M},\mu)
is equi-integrable if and only if there exists a function
H:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying

\tfrac{H(r)}{r}\rightarrow+\infty\ as\ r\rightarrow+\infty

and such that

\sup\limits_{f\in\mathscr{F}}\int_{X}H(f)d\mu<+\infty.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
J
be a discrete or continuous set of indicators. Given
M_{1},M_{2}>0
, assume that
\{f_{j}(x,\xi,I):j\in J\}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})_{+}
satisfies

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)f_{j}dxd\xi dI\leq M_{1}\,,\ \iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{j}|\log f_{j}|dxd\xi dI\leq M_{2}

for all
j\in J
. Then
\{f_{j}(x,\xi,I):j\in J\}
is relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

Proof.

It’s not hard to find
f_{j}
is bounded in
L^{1}
. Moreover, for any
\epsilon>0
, there must exist
R>e^{\frac{2M_{2}}{\epsilon}}>0
such that
\log f_{j}>\frac{2M_{2}}{\epsilon}
, when
f_{j}>R
. Let
\delta=\frac{\epsilon}{2R}
. Then for any
j\in J
and measurable set
E
with
\mu(E)<\delta
, one has

\displaystyle\iiint_{E}f_{j}dxd\xi dI=

\displaystyle\iiint_{E\cap\{f_{j}\leq R\}}f_{j}dxd\xi dI+\iiint_{E\cap\{f_{j}>R\}}f_{j}dxd\xi dI

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle R\mu(E)+\iiint_{E\cap\{f_{j}>R\}}\tfrac{1}{|\log f_{j}|}f_{j}|\log f_{j}|dxd\xi dI\leq\epsilon.

Besides, for any
\epsilon>0
, there must exist
R>0
such that
(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)^{-1}<\frac{\epsilon}{M_{1}}
when
|x|>R
. Let
K=\{x:|x|\leq R\}
. Then for any
j\in J
, one has

\iiint_{K^{c}}f_{j}dxd\xi dI=\iiint_{K^{c}}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)^{-1}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)f_{j}dxd\xi dI\leq\epsilon.

By Dunford-Pettis Theorem (Lemma
2.1
), we know
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J}
is relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Then the proof of Lemma
2.3
is finished.
∎

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
J
be a discrete or continuous set of indicators. Given
M_{1},M_{2}>0
, assume that
\{f_{j}(x,\xi):j\in J\}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})_{+}
satisfies

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})f_{j}dxd\xi\leq M_{1},\ \iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{j}|\log f_{j}|dxd\xi\leq M_{2}

for all
j\in J
. Then
\{f_{j}(x,\xi):j\in J\}
is relatively weakly compact in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

We now introduce the following weighted
L^{1}
spaces.

Definition 2.2

(DiPerna-Lions’ work
[
17
]
)
.

If
f(x,\xi)
is a measurable function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, denote by

\|f\|_{1,2}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})|f(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\,.

Define the space
L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
by

L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})=\{f(x,\xi)\textrm{ measurable on }\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3};\|f\|_{1,2}<\infty\}

endowed with the
L^{1}_{2}
-norm
\|f\|_{1,2}
.

Definition 2.3
.

If
f(x,\xi,I)
is a measurable function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, denote by

\|f\|_{1,3}=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}(1+|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)|f(x,\xi,I)|dxd\xi dI\,.

Define the space
L^{1}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
by

L^{1}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})=\{f(x,\xi,I)\textrm{ measurable on }\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+};\|f\|_{1,3}<\infty\}

endowed with the
L^{1}_{3}
-norm
\|f\|_{1,3}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume
f_{\alpha}(x,\xi,I)\in L^{1}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})_{+}
and
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
. Then

\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})dxd\xi dI<+\infty

if and only if

f_{\alpha}\log^{+}(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Proof.

Firstly, the necessity is obvious. On the contrary, if
f_{\alpha}\log^{+}(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we need to prove
\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})dxd\xi dI<+\infty
. It suffices to prove
f_{\alpha}\log^{-}(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
. In fact, using the inequality
u^{\frac{1}{2}}\log u^{-1}<1(0<u<1)
, one has

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}\log^{-}(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})dxd\xi dI

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha}\log\tfrac{1}{I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{0<I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha}<\mathrm{exp}-(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)}dxd\xi dI

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}\log\tfrac{1}{I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{exp}-(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)<I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha}<1}dxd\xi dI

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}\mathrm{exp}(-\tfrac{1}{2}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I))dxd\xi dI+\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}+I)f_{\alpha}dxd\xi dI<+\infty.

Therefore, the proof of Lemma
2.4
is finished.
∎

Corollary 2.2
.

Assume
f_{\alpha}(x,\xi)\in L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})_{+}
and
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
. Then

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}\log f_{\alpha}dxd\xi<+\infty\textrm{ if and only if }f_{\alpha}\log^{+}f_{\alpha}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})\,.

Lemma 2.5

(Product Limit Theorem,
[
9
,
17
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a finite, positive Borel measure on a Borel subset
X
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Assume that
f^{n},f\in L^{1}(d\mu)
and
g^{n},g\in L^{\infty}(d\mu)
satisfy

(1)

w-\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}f^{n}=f
in
L^{1}(d\mu)
;

(2)

\{g^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(d\mu)
and
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}g^{n}=g,a.e.

Then
w-\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}f^{n}g^{n}=fg
in
L^{1}(d\mu)
, where “
w-\lim
” means weak limit.

Now we give the next two lemmas, which will be used to construct the approximate initial data.

Lemma 2.6
.

Assume that
f^{n}_{\alpha}(x,\xi)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})_{+}
with
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
satisfy

(1)

there exists a constant
M>0
such that

\|f^{n}_{\alpha}\|_{1,2}\leq M,\ f^{n}_{\alpha}\log f^{n}_{\alpha}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3});

(2)

\{f^{n}_{\alpha}(x,\xi)\}
converges to
f_{\alpha}(x,\xi)
weakly in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Moreover, assume that
f^{n}_{\alpha}(x,\xi,I)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})_{+}
with
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
satisfy

(3)

there exists a constant
M>0
such that

\|f^{n}_{\alpha}\|_{1,3}\leq M,\ f^{n}_{\alpha}\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f^{n}_{\alpha})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+});

(4)

\{f^{n}_{\alpha}(x,\xi,I)\}
converges to
f_{\alpha}(x,\xi,I)
weakly in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

Then

(2.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{s_{0}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{\alpha}\log f_{\alpha}dxd\xi+\sum_{\alpha=s_{0}+1}^{s}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f_{\alpha}\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f_{\alpha})dxd\xi dI

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Big\{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{s_{0}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f^{n}_{\alpha}\log f^{n}_{\alpha}dxd\xi+\sum_{\alpha=s_{0}+1}^{s}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}f^{n}_{\alpha}\log(I^{1-\delta(\alpha)/2}f^{n}_{\alpha})dxd\xi dI\Big\}.

The proof of Lemma
2.6
will be given in Section
8
later.

2.2.
Collision invariants

The results in this subsection follow directly from the Section 2.2 of
[
12
]
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For all
\{\alpha,\beta\}\subset\{1,\cdots,s\}
, the following measures are invariant under the changes (
1.29
):

\displaystyle d\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}=

\displaystyle W_{\alpha\beta}(\xi,\xi_{*},I,I_{*})d\xi d\xi_{*}d\xi^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}_{*}dIdI_{*}dI^{\prime}dI^{\prime}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &B_{0\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},\omega)d\omega d\xi_{*}d\xi\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{m}\,,\\
&B_{1\alpha\beta}(\xi-\xi_{*},I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\omega)(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{\frac{1}{2}}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}d\omega d\mathfrak{R}d\xi_{*}dI_{*}d\xi\,,\ \alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{m},\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{p}\,,\\
&B_{1\beta\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{*},I,\mathfrak{R},\omega)(1-\mathfrak{R})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{\frac{1}{2}}I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}d\omega d\mathfrak{R}d\xi_{*}d\xi dI\,,\quad\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{p},\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{m}\,,\\
&B(\xi-\xi_{*},I+I_{*},\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{r},\omega)\mathfrak{r}^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(1-\mathfrak{r})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}\mathfrak{R}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\ \times(1-\mathfrak{R})^{(\delta(\alpha)+\delta(\beta))/2-1}I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}d\omega d\mathfrak{r}d\mathfrak{R}d\xi_{*}dI_{*}d\xi dI\,,\quad\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{I}_{p}\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{I}_{m}=\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and
\mathcal{I}_{p}=\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
.

The weak form of the collision operator
Q(f,f)
reads

(Q(f,f),g)=\sum\limits_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{s}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})^{4}}(\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})g_{\alpha}d\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}

for any function
g=(g_{1},\cdots,g_{s})
, with
g_{\alpha}=g_{\alpha}(\mathbf{Z})
. Then by the Lemma
2.7
, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
g=(g_{1},\cdots,g_{s})
, with
g_{\alpha}=g_{\alpha}(\mathbf{Z})
, it follows that

(Q(f,f),g)=\tfrac{1}{4}\sum\limits_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{s}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})^{4}}(\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})\Delta_{\alpha\beta}(g)d\Gamma_{\alpha\beta},

where
\Delta_{\alpha\beta}(g):=g_{\alpha}+g_{\beta_{*}}-g_{\alpha}^{\prime}-g^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}
.

Definition 2.4
.

A vector function
g=(g_{1},\cdots,g_{s})
, with
g_{\alpha}=(\mathbf{Z})
is a collision invariant if

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})^{4}}(\tfrac{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})\Delta_{\alpha\beta}(g)d\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}=0,

for all
\{\alpha,\beta\}\subset\{1,\cdots,s\}
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\cdots,m_{s}),\mathbf{I}=(\underbrace{0,\cdots,0}_{s_{0}},\underbrace{I,\cdots,I}_{s_{1}})
, and
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{s}\}
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{s}
. Then the vector space of collision invariants is generated by

\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{s},\mathbf{m}\xi_{x},\mathbf{m}\xi_{y},\mathbf{m}\xi_{z},\mathbf{m}|\xi|^{2}+2\mathbf{I}\}.

Define

(2.2)

W[f]:=(Q(f,f),\log(\varphi^{-1}f)),

where
\varphi=diag(I^{\delta(1)/2-1},\cdots,I^{\delta(s)/2-1})
. It follows by Proposition
2.1
that

(2.3)

\displaystyle W[f]=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{4}\sum\limits_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{s}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})^{4}}(\tfrac{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-1)

\displaystyle\times\log(\tfrac{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}d\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}.

It is known that
(x-1)\log x\geq 0
for
x\geq 0
, which means that
W[f]
is non-positive, that is
W[f]\leq 0.
Then, by means of the weak formulation, the entropy identity is obtained by multiplying (
1.1
) by
1+\log(\varphi^{-1}f)
, integrating over
\mathbf{Z}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
and using (
1.29
) that

(2.4)

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dt}H(f)(t)+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum\limits_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{s}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})^{4}}(\tfrac{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}-1)

\displaystyle\times\log(\tfrac{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}f^{\prime}_{\alpha}f^{\prime}_{\beta_{*}}}{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}(I^{\prime})^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}(I^{\prime}_{*})^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}})\tfrac{f_{\alpha}f_{\beta_{*}}}{I^{\delta(\alpha)/2-1}I_{*}^{\delta(\beta)/2-1}}d\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}=0\,,

where
H(f)
is defined in (
1.45
).

2.3.
Equivalent description of renormalized solutions

In this subsection, we will give the definitions of distributional, mild and renormalized solutions and the connection among them. The main results and methods of proof are taken from
[
17
]
. Besides, throughout this section,
we will denote by
f_{\alpha}
a nonnegative element of
L^{1}_{loc}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
for all
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and a nonnegative element of
L^{1}_{loc}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
for all
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
.

Definition 2.5

(Distributional solution)
.

We say that
f=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
is a distributional solution of (
1.1
) if it satisfies

(1)

for
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
,
f_{\alpha}
solves

\tfrac{\partial f_{\alpha}}{\partial t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{\alpha}=Q_{\alpha}(f,f),\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3});

(2)

for
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
,
f_{\alpha}
solves

(2.5)

\tfrac{\partial f_{\alpha}}{\partial t}+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{\alpha}=Q_{\alpha}(f,f),\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Definition 2.6

(Mild solution)
.

We say that
f=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{s})
is a mild solution of (
1.1
) if it satisfies

(1)

for
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and for almost all
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3},
one has
Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)^{\sharp}(t,x,\xi)\in L^{1}(0,T)
for all
T<\infty
, and

\displaystyle f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\xi)-f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(s,x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}Q_{\alpha}(f,f)^{\sharp}(\sigma,x,\xi)d\sigma\quad(\forall 0<s<t<\infty),

where
f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\xi)=f_{\alpha}(t,x+t\xi,\xi)
;

(2)

for
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
and for almost all
(x,\xi,I)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},
there hold
Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)^{\sharp}(t,x,\xi,I)\in L^{1}(0,T)
for all
T<\infty
, and

(2.6)

\displaystyle f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\xi,I)-f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(s,x,\xi,I)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}Q_{\alpha}(f,f)^{\sharp}(\sigma,x,\xi,I)d\sigma\quad(\forall 0<s<t<\infty),

where
f^{\sharp}_{\alpha}(t,x,\xi,I)=f_{\alpha}(t,x+t\xi,\xi,I)
.

Next we study the connection among the distributional, mild and renormalized solutions.

Lemma 2.8
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

If
Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}_{loc}
, then
f
is a distributional solution of (
1.1
) if and only if
f
is a renormalized solution of (
1.1
);

(2)

f
is a renormalized solution of (
1.1
) if and only if for all
\beta\in C^{1}[0,+\infty)
such that
|\beta^{\prime}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{1+t}
for some
C>0
,
\beta(f_{\alpha})
enjoys

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\beta(f_{\alpha})+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta(f_{\alpha})=\beta^{\prime}(f_{\alpha})Q_{\alpha}(f,f)\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})

for all
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s_{0}\}
and
\beta^{\prime}(f_{\alpha})Q_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}_{loc}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\beta(f_{\alpha})+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta(f_{\alpha})=\beta^{\prime}(f_{\alpha})Q_{\alpha}(f,f),\quad\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

for all
\alpha\in\{s_{0}+1,\cdots,s\}
and
\beta^{\prime}(f_{\alpha})Q_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}_{loc}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as Lemma II.1 of
[
17
]
. We omit the details here for simplicity.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

For
\alpha\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
, the following statements hold:

(1)

If
Q^{\pm}(f,f)\in L^{1}_{loc}
, then
f
is a distributional solution of (
1.1
) if and only if
f
is a mild solution of (
1.1
);

(2)

f
is a renormalized solution of (
1.1
) if and only if
f
is a mild solution of (
1.1
) and
(1+f_{\alpha})^{-1}Q^{\pm}_{\alpha}(f,f)\in L^{1}_{loc}
.

Proof.
